<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We study tree norms computed by two least nonnegative fields whose difference is the vector. For Euclidean child aggregation, two root-sum spaces have an averaged asymptotic midpoint modulus of at least $`t^3/128`$ for $`0\lt t\lt 1`$, including a reflexive joining-root space. A reflexive construction with a fixed zero root also satisfies a homogeneous cubic estimate. These spaces admit no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm. We also obtain sixth-power and cubic estimates when the aggregation exponent depends on the height of a finite component.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-rec-joined-root-stability)): **Theorem 1**. *For $N=N_\Sigma$ on $X_\Sigma$, or $N=N_J$ on $X_J$, one has $$\label{rec:sum-modulus} \widehat\delta_N(t)\ge t^3/128\qquad(0<t<1).$$ For $X_0$, a finite initial set $H$, a vector $x$ supported in $H$, and $z\in F_H\setminus\{0\}$, one has $$\label{rec:equal-estimate} \frac{N_0(x+z)+N_0(x-z)}2\ge N_0(x) +\frac{N_0(z)^3}{8(2N_0(x)+N_0(z))^2}.$$ Each of $X_\Sigma,X_0,X_J$ admits no equivalent AUC norm.*
  - Explanation: Establishes averaged cubic midpoint bounds for the root-sum norms, a homogeneous bound for the common-root norm, and the obstruction to equivalent AUC norms.
- **C2** (construction, [source](#stmt-rec-completion)): These formulas define infinite-dimensional Banach spaces after completion. Coordinate evaluations and the fields extend continuously. If $H$ is a finite set containing the root and all predecessors of its nodes, then coordinate restriction $\Pi_H$ is a contractive projection. Its kernel $F_H$ has finite codimension, and finitely supported elements of $F_H$ are dense in $F_H$. Both $X_0$ and $X_J$ are reflexive.
  - Explanation: Establishes the completions, continuous fields, contractive initial projections, dense finite tails, and reflexivity of the joined spaces.
- **C3** (bound, [source](#stmt-rec-two-field)): Let $J$ be a subset of the leaves of a finite tree with root $o$. Suppose that $P,Q,P',Q'$ are nonnegative fields, each satisfying $$A_s^2\ge\sum_{c\in\operatorname{ch}(s)}A_c^2$$ at every internal node, for each $A\in\{P,Q,P',Q'\}$. Suppose also that $P'-P=Q'-Q=d\ge0$, and that at each internal node at least one of the inequalities for $P,Q$ is an equality. If $P_b=Q_b=0$ for $b\in J$, then $$\sum_{b\in J}d_b^2 \le\bigl[d_o(2P_o+d_o)(2Q_o+d_o)\bigr]^{2/3}.$$
  - Explanation: Propagates boundary increment energy to the root using the tightness of a base-field child inequality.
- **C4** (bound, [source](#stmt-rec-root-sum-stability)): Let $N=N_\Sigma$ or $N=N_J$. Suppose that $x$ is a finitely supported unit vector and that $z\in F_H$, where $H$ is a finite initial set containing $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}x$. If $$d=\frac12\left(\frac{N(x+z)+N(x-z)}2-1\right),$$ then $d\ge0$ and $N(z)^3\le64d(1+d)^2$.
  - Explanation: Controls a tail outside an initial head by the averaged root defect in either root-sum convention.
- **C5** (theorem, [source](#stmt-rec-bounded-tree)): Let $X$ be a Banach space. Suppose that for every $h\ge1$ there are vectors $u_s^{(h)}$, indexed by $s\in\mathbb N^{\le h}$, such that $0<m\le\\|u_s^{(h)}\\|\le M<\infty$ uniformly. Suppose also that for $\|s\|<h$ the increments $d_{s,k}^{(h)}=u_{s^\frown k}^{(h)}-u_s^{(h)}$ are weakly null as $k\to\infty$ and have norms at least $c>0$, uniformly in $h,s,k$. If $N$ is a norm and $0<\alpha\le\beta<\infty$ satisfy $\alpha\\|\cdot\\|\le N\le\beta\\|\cdot\\|$, then $$\overline\delta_N\left(\frac{\alpha c}{2\beta M}\right)=0.$$ The conclusion also holds if one infinite tree has these properties.
  - Explanation: Uses arbitrarily long bounded paths with weakly null increments to obstruct one-sided asymptotic convexity under equivalent norms.
- **C6** (theorem, [source](#stmt-stop-sequential)): For each $n$, let $x_n,z_n$ be finitely supported real vectors on $\mathbb N^{<\omega}$, with $N_\Sigma(x_n)=1$. Let $H_n$ be the predecessor closure of $\operatorname{supp}x_n$, and suppose that $(z_n)_s=0$ for every $s\in H_n$. Then $$\frac{N_\Sigma(x_n+z_n)+N_\Sigma(x_n-z_n)}2\longrightarrow1 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad N_\Sigma(z_n)\longrightarrow0.$$ In particular, the assertion is uniform in the height and size of $H_n$.
  - Explanation: Shows that vanishing midpoint excess forces the tail norm to vanish uniformly over finite heads.
- **C7** (theorem, [source](#stmt-stop-all-radii)): For every $t>0$, the averaged asymptotic modulus of $N_\Sigma$ is positive.
  - Explanation: Extends positive averaged midpoint separation to every positive radius.
- **C8** (bound, [source](#stmt-log-finite-tree)): Fix $M\ge\beta>0$. Let $p,q,r$ be nonnegative fields on a finite rooted tree with root $o$. Suppose that $p,q,p+r,q+r$ are upper fields, and at every nonleaf the upper inequality for at least one of $p,q$ is an equality. If $$p_o+q_o+2r_o\le M,\qquad \sum_{b\text{ a leaf}}r_b^2\ge\beta^2,$$ then $r_o\ge\gamma(M,\beta)>0$, independently of the tree. One possible choice is $$\label{log:gamma} \begin{gathered} h=\frac{\beta^2}{2M^2},\qquad c_* =\frac{\beta\sqrt h}{8},\qquad H_*=1+\log(M/c_*),\qquad K=\frac{4(2+\sqrt6)H_*}{h},\\ \gamma(M,\beta)=\frac{\beta h}{8}e^{-K}. \end{gathered}$$
  - Explanation: Provides an explicit positive root gap from nonzero leaf energy, uniformly over finite trees.
- **C9** (bound, [source](#stmt-log-tail-gap)): Let $x,y$ be finitely supported vectors in $X_J$. Suppose that $x$ is supported in a finite predecessor-closed set $H$ containing the joining root, and that $y$ vanishes on $H$. If $$N_J(x+y),N_J(x-y)\le2, \qquad N_J(y)\ge\zeta,\qquad 0<\zeta\le2,$$ then $$\frac{N_J(x+y)+N_J(x-y)}2 \ge N_J(x)+2\gamma(2,\zeta/4).$$
  - Explanation: Converts the logarithmic upper-field gap into a finite-head midpoint gap for the joined-root sum.
- **C10** (theorem, [source](#stmt-log-lens-gap)): For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is $\eta(\varepsilon)\in(0,1)$ such that, for $x,z_1,z_2,\ldots\in X_J$, $$\left. \begin{gathered} N_J(x+z_j),N_J(x-z_j)\le1\quad(j\ge1),\\ N_J(z_i-z_j)\ge\varepsilon\quad(i\ne j) \end{gathered} \right\} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad N_J(x)\le1-\eta(\varepsilon).$$ For $0<\varepsilon\le2$, one can take $\eta(\varepsilon)=\min\{1/2,\gamma(2,\varepsilon/16)\}$.
  - Explanation: Bounds the center norm away from the unit sphere for an infinite separated family of midpoint perturbations.
- **C11** (construction, [source](#stmt-var-space)): The recursion <a href="#var:recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:recursion">[var:recursion]</a> defines a norm equivalent to the usual $\ell_{p_h}(T_h)$ norm. In particular, $E_h$ is complete and reflexive, with separable dual. The space $X_{\mathrm v}$ is complete and reflexive, its dual is separable, and vectors with finite total coordinate support are dense in it. Every finite coordinate projection is bounded and has finite rank.
  - Explanation: Identifies the component completion and establishes reflexivity, separable duals, finite-support density, and bounded finite projections.
- **C12** (theorem, [source](#stmt-var-no-auc)): The space $X_{\mathrm v}$ admits no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm. More precisely, if $a\\|\cdot\\|_{\mathrm v}\le N\le b\\|\cdot\\|_{\mathrm v}$ with $0<a\le b$, then $\overline\delta_N(a/b)=0$.
  - Explanation: Excludes equivalent AUC norms for the variable-exponent outer sum by bounded paths with weakly null increments.
- **C13** (bound, [source](#stmt-var-sixth-component)): Let $x,y\in E_h$, with $x$ supported on a finite ancestral set $A$ and $y$ zero on $A$. Put $B=\nu_h(x)$, $Y=\nu_h(y)$, and $M_\sigma=\nu_h(x+\sigma y)$. With $\mathrm e=\exp(1)$ and $$c_6=2\exp(-(\mathrm e-1))4^{-6},\qquad U^2=M_+^2+M_-^2,$$ one has $$\label{var:sixth-component-eq} U^2-2B^2\ge c_6 U^{-4}Y^6.$$ A quotient with numerator and denominator both zero is interpreted as zero. The same estimate holds when $x=0$ and $y$ is arbitrary.
  - Explanation: Controls the sixth power of a component tail by the excess sum of squared endpoint norms.
- **C14** (theorem, [source](#stmt-var-sixth-stability)): Let $x\in X_{\mathrm v}$ have finite total support. In each component where $x^{(h)}\ne0$, choose a finite ancestral set $A_h$ containing that support. Suppose $y\in X_{\mathrm v}$ vanishes on every such $A_h$. With $S=\\|x+y\\|_{\mathrm v}^2+\\|x-y\\|_{\mathrm v}^2$, one has $$\label{var:sixth-sum} S-2\\|x\\|_{\mathrm v}^2 \ge c_6 S^{-2}\\|y\\|_{\mathrm v}^6, \qquad c_6=2\exp(-(\mathrm e-1))4^{-6}.$$ The right side is zero when $S=0$.
  - Explanation: Aggregates the sixth-power component bound over the outer Hilbert sum without height-dependent constants.
- **C15** (bound, [source](#stmt-var-cubic-component)): Let $x,y\in E_h$, with $x$ supported on a finite ancestral set $A$ and $y$ zero on $A$. Put $M_\sigma=\nu_h(x+\sigma y)$, $B=\nu_h(x)$, and $M=\max\{M_+,M_-\}$. If $M>0$, then $$\label{var:cubic-component-eq} \frac{M_++M_-}{2}-B \ge c_3\frac{\nu_h(y)^3}{M^2},\qquad c_3=(6\mathrm e)^{-3}.$$ This also holds for $x=0$ and arbitrary $y$. When $M=0$, both vectors are zero.
  - Explanation: Accumulates exit energy across levels to obtain a cubic component midpoint estimate.
- **C16** (theorem, [source](#stmt-var-cubic-stability)): Let $x\in X_{\mathrm v}$ have finite total support. In every component with $x^{(h)}\ne0$, choose a finite ancestral set $A_h$ containing its support, and let $y\in X_{\mathrm v}$ vanish on these sets. If $R>0$ and $\\|x+y\\|_{\mathrm v},\\|x-y\\|_{\mathrm v}\le R$, then $$\label{var:cubic-sum} R^2\ge\\|x\\|_{\mathrm v}^2+ \frac{c_3}{2\sqrt2\,R}\\|y\\|_{\mathrm v}^3, \qquad c_3=(6\mathrm e)^{-3}.$$
  - Explanation: Aggregates the cubic component estimate into a ball-radius bound for finite heads and completed tails.
- **C17** (theorem, [source](#stmt-var-cubic-weak-null)): Let $x\in X_{\mathrm v}$ and $0<\varepsilon\le1$. Suppose $(y_j)$ is weakly null, $\\|y_j\\|_{\mathrm v}\ge\varepsilon$, and $\\|x+y_j\\|_{\mathrm v},\\|x-y_j\\|_{\mathrm v}\le1$ for every $j$. Then $$\label{var:theta} \\|x\\|_{\mathrm v}\le\theta(\varepsilon) :=\left(1-\frac{c_3}{2\sqrt2}\varepsilon^3\right)^{1/2}<1.$$
  - Explanation: Removes the finite-support restriction on the center for weakly null midpoint perturbations.

      "exact_statement": "These formulas define infinite-dimensional Banach spaces after completion. Coordinate evaluations and the fields extend continuously.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-rec-two-field",
        "exact_quote": "Let $J$ be a subset of the leaves of a finite tree with root $o$. Suppose that $P,Q,P',Q'$ are nonnegative fields, each satisfying $$A_s^2\\ge\\sum_{c\\in\\operatorname{ch}(s)}A_c^2$$ at every internal node, for each $A\\in\\{P,Q,P',Q'\\}$. Suppose also that $P'-P=Q'-Q=d\\ge0$, and that at each internal node at least one of the inequalities for $P,Q$ is an equality. If $P_b=Q_b=0$ for $b\\in J$, then $$\\sum_{b\\in J}d_b^2\n \\le\\bigl[d_o(2P_o+d_o)(2Q_o+d_o)\\bigr]^{2/3}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-331.7"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-rec-bounded-tree",
        "exact_quote": "Let $X$ be a Banach space. Suppose that for every $h\\ge1$ there are vectors $u_s^{(h)}$, indexed by $s\\in\\mathbb N^{\\le h}$, such that $0<m\\le\\|u_s^{(h)}\\|\\le M<\\infty$ uniformly. Suppose also that for $|s|<h$ the increments $d_{s,k}^{(h)}=u_{s^\\frown k}^{(h)}-u_s^{(h)}$ are weakly null as $k\\to\\infty$ and have norms at least $c>0$, uniformly in $h,s,k$. If $N$ is a norm and $0<\\alpha\\le\\beta<\\infty$ satisfy $\\alpha\\|\\cdot\\|\\le N\\le\\beta\\|\\cdot\\|$, then $$\\overline\\delta_N\\left(\\frac{\\alpha c}{2\\beta M}\\right)=0.$$ The conclusion also holds if one infinite tree has these properties.",
        "source_id": "PM-331.7"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-stop-sequential",
        "exact_quote": "For each $n$, let $x_n,z_n$ be finitely supported real vectors on $\\mathbb N^{<\\omega}$, with $N_\\Sigma(x_n)=1$. Let $H_n$ be the predecessor closure of $\\operatorname{supp}x_n$, and suppose that $(z_n)_s=0$ for every $s\\in H_n$. Then $$\\frac{N_\\Sigma(x_n+z_n)+N_\\Sigma(x_n-z_n)}2\\longrightarrow1\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad N_\\Sigma(z_n)\\longrightarrow0.$$ In particular, the assertion is uniform in the height and size of $H_n$.",
        "source_id": "PM-331.7"

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-log-finite-tree",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $M\\ge\\beta>0$. Let $p,q,r$ be nonnegative fields on a finite rooted tree with root $o$. Suppose that $p,q,p+r,q+r$ are upper fields, and at every nonleaf the upper inequality for at least one of $p,q$ is an equality. If $$p_o+q_o+2r_o\\le M,\\qquad \\sum_{b\\text{ a leaf}}r_b^2\\ge\\beta^2,$$ then $r_o\\ge\\gamma(M,\\beta)>0$, independently of the tree. One possible choice is $$\\label{log:gamma}\n \\begin{gathered}\n h=\\frac{\\beta^2}{2M^2},\\qquad c_* =\\frac{\\beta\\sqrt h}{8},\\qquad\n H_*=1+\\log(M/c_*),\\qquad\n K=\\frac{4(2+\\sqrt6)H_*}{h},\\\\\n \\gamma(M,\\beta)=\\frac{\\beta h}{8}e^{-K}.\n \\end{gathered}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-331.7"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-log-finite-tree",
          "exact_quote": "Fix $M\\ge\\beta>0$. Let $p,q,r$ be nonnegative fields on a finite rooted tree with root $o$.           "source_id": "PM-331.7"
        }

        "exact_quote": "Let $x,y$ be finitely supported vectors in $X_J$.           "anchor": "stmt-log-tail-gap",
          "exact_quote": "Let $x,y$ be finitely supported vectors in $X_J$. Suppose that $x$ is supported in a finite predecessor-closed set $H$ containing the joining root, and that $y$ vanishes on $H$. If $$N_J(x+y),N_J(x-y)\\le2,\n \\qquad N_J(y)\\ge\\zeta,\\qquad 0<\\zeta\\le2,$$ then $$\\frac{N_J(x+y)+N_J(x-y)}2\n \\ge N_J(x)+2\\gamma(2,\\zeta/4).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-331.7"
        }
      ],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-log-tail-gap"
    },
    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-log-lens-gap",
        "exact_quote": "For every $\\varepsilon>0$ there is $\\eta(\\varepsilon)\\in(0,1)$ such that, for $x,z_1,z_2,\\ldots\\in X_J$, $$\\left.\n \\begin{gathered}\n N_J(x+z_j),N_J(x-z_j)\\le1\\quad(j\\ge1),\\\\\n N_J(z_i-z_j)\\ge\\varepsilon\\quad(i\\ne j)\n \\end{gathered}\n \\right\\}\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad N_J(x)\\le1-\\eta(\\varepsilon).$$ For $0<\\varepsilon\\le2$, one can take $\\eta(\\varepsilon)=\\min\\{1/2,\\gamma(2,\\varepsilon/16)\\}$.",
        "source_id": "PM-331.7"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-var-space",
        "exact_quote": "The recursion <a href=\"#var:recursion\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"var:recursion\">[var:recursion]</a> defines a norm equivalent to the usual $\\ell_{p_h}(T_h)$ norm. In particular, $E_h$ is complete and reflexive, with separable dual. The space $X_{\\mathrm v}$ is complete and reflexive, its dual is separable, and vectors with finite total coordinate support are dense in it. Every finite coordinate projection is bounded and has finite rank.",
        "source_id": "PM-331.7"
      },

        "exact_quote": "Let $x\\in X_{\\mathrm v}$ have finite total support. In each component where $x^{(h)}\\ne0$, choose a finite ancestral set $A_h$ containing that support.         "exact_quote": "Let $x\\in X_{\\mathrm v}$ and $0<\\varepsilon\\le1$. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Closed finite-codimensional subspaces** ([source](#intro:modulus)): For an infinite-dimensional real Banach space $X$ with norm $N$, let $\operatorname{cof}(X)$ denote its closed finite-codimensional linear subspaces.
- **Averaged asymptotic midpoint modulus** ([source](#intro:modulus)): The averaged asymptotic midpoint modulus is $$\label{intro:modulus} \widehat\delta_N(t)=\inf_{N(x)=1}\sup_{F\in\operatorname{cof}(X)} \inf_{\substack{y\in F\\N(y)\ge1}} \left(\frac{N(x+ty)+N(x-ty)}2-1\right),\qquad t>0.$$
- **Asymptotic midpoint uniform convexity** ([source](#intro:modulus)): The norm is *asymptotically midpoint uniformly convex*, abbreviated AMUC, if $m_N(t)>0$ for every $t>0$.
- **One-sided asymptotic modulus** ([source](#intro:modulus)): The one-sided modulus is $$\label{intro:auc} \overline\delta_N(t)=\inf_{N(x)=1}\sup_{F\in\operatorname{cof}(X)} \inf_{\substack{y\in F\\N(y)=1}}\bigl(N(x+ty)-1\bigr).$$
- **Least Euclidean fields** ([source](#rec:definition)): For a finitely supported vector $v=(v_s)$, set the fields to zero below its support and compute upwards: $$\begin{aligned} A_s(v)&=\Bigl(\sum_{c\in\operatorname{ch}(s)}P_c(v)^2\Bigr)^{1/2},& B_s(v)&=\Bigl(\sum_{c\in\operatorname{ch}(s)}Q_c(v)^2\Bigr)^{1/2},\notag\\ P_s(v)&=\max\{A_s(v),v_s+B_s(v)\},& Q_s(v)&=\max\{B_s(v),-v_s+A_s(v)\}. \label{rec:definition} \end{aligned}$$
- **Infinite-tree root-sum norm** ([source](#pm-h-5)): On $T=\mathbb N^{<\omega}$, including the root $o=\varnothing$, put $$N_\Sigma(v)=P_o(v)+Q_o(v),\qquad v\in c_{00}(T),$$ and let $X_\Sigma$ be the completion.
- **Common zero-root norm** ([source](#pm-h-5)): Let $T_n=\bigcup_{j=0}^n\mathbb N^j$, $n\ge1$, be disjoint copies with roots $r_n$. Add a root $\rho$ whose children are the $r_n$, obtaining a tree $\mathcal J$. For vectors on $\mathcal J\setminus\{\rho\}$ set $v_\rho=0$. Then $P_\rho(v)=Q_\rho(v)$; define $$N_0(v)=P_\rho(v)=Q_\rho(v),$$ and let $X_0$ be the completion.
- **Free joining-root sum norm** ([source](#pm-h-5)): For a free root coordinate $a=v_\rho$, with the remaining coordinates written as $w$, let $A=(\sum_nP_{r_n}(w)^2)^{1/2}$ and $B=(\sum_nQ_{r_n}(w)^2)^{1/2}$. Then $$\label{rec:free-root-equivalence} N_J(v)=\max\{2A-a,2B+a\},\qquad \max\{\|a\|,L(w)\}\le N_J(v)\le \|a\|+2L(w).$$
- **Initial head and tail subspace** ([source](#pm-h-5)): We call a finite root-containing set *initial* if it contains all predecessors of its nodes, write $\Pi_H$ for coordinate restriction, and put $F_H=\ker\Pi_H$ in the corresponding completion.
- **Upper field** ([source](#log:section)): Call a nonnegative field $a$ on a finite rooted tree an *upper field* if $a_s^2\ge\sum_{c\in\operatorname{ch}(s)}a_c^2$ at every nonleaf.
- **Variable-exponent component and outer sum** ([source](#pm-h-12)): Define $$\nu_h(v)=P_o(v)+Q_o(v),\qquad E_h=(\ell_{p_h}(T_h),\nu_h),\qquad X_{\mathrm v}=\Bigl(\bigoplus_{h=1}^{\infty}E_h\Bigr)_{\ell_2}.$$
- **Ancestral head** ([source](#var:common-increments)): A finite set $A\subset T_h$ is *ancestral* if it contains the root and every prefix of each of its elements.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | Averaged root-sum midpoint modulus | ≥ | $t^3/128$ | Positive radii below the unit radius; either root-sum norm. |
| Q2 | Common-root midpoint average gain above the center norm | ≥ | $\frac{N_0(z)^3}{8(2N_0(x)+N_0(z))^2}$ | Finite initial head, center supported in it, and a nonzero tail; the displayed fraction is the gain above the center norm. |
| Q3 | Averaged versus maximum midpoint modulus | ≥ | $\tfrac12m_N(t)$ | Positive radii. |
| Q4 | Cubed tail norm | ≤ | $64d(1+d)^2$ | A finite unit center, an initial head containing its support, and a tail in the head kernel; the defect is defined in the statement. |
| Q5 | Child aggregation exponent | = | $1+\frac1h$ | An integer component height; the exponent remains constant within that component. |
| Q6 | Logarithmic propagation gap | = | $\frac{\beta h}{8}e^{-K}$ | Positive leaf threshold bounded by the root budget; auxiliary parameters are those defined in the statement. |
| Q7 | Sixth-power stability coefficient | = | $2\exp(-(\mathrm e-1))4^{-6}$ | A center supported in a finite ancestral set and a perturbation vanishing there; the zero-center case is included. |
| Q8 | Cubic stability coefficient | = | $(6\mathrm e)^{-3}$ | Finite ancestral head with a tail vanishing on it; positive endpoint maximum, with the zero case stated separately. |
| Q9 | Weakly null midpoint center bound | ≤ | $\left(1-\frac{c_3}{2\sqrt2}\varepsilon^3\right)^{1/2}$ | Weakly null perturbations bounded below in norm with both endpoints in the unit ball; the separation parameter is positive and at most the unit radius. |
| Q10 | Variable-exponent averaged midpoint modulus | ≥ | $\frac{c_3}{24\sqrt2}\,t^3$ | Positive radii below the unit radius. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-rec-joined-root-stability) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rec-root-sum-stability): Boundary energy, finite approximation, and bounded paths -> Establishes averaged cubic midpoint bounds for the root-sum norms, a homogeneous bound for the common-root norm, and the obstruction to equivalent AUC norms.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-rec-completion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rec-completion): Minimal fields, coordinate projections, and dual identification -> Establishes the completions, continuous fields, contractive initial projections, dense finite tails, and reflexivity of the joined spaces.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-rec-two-field) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rec-two-field): Hölder propagation with a tight base field -> Propagates boundary increment energy to the root using the tightness of a base-field child inequality.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-rec-root-sum-stability) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rec-root-sum-stability): Boundary-to-root conversion and tail density -> Controls a tail outside an initial head by the averaged root defect in either root-sum convention.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-rec-bounded-tree) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rec-bounded-tree): Finite-codimensional approximation and bounded-path contradiction -> Uses arbitrarily long bounded paths with weakly null increments to obstruct one-sided asymptotic convexity under equivalent norms.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-stop-sequential) / [proof](#proof-stmt-stop-sequential): Stopping products and a predictable martingale transform -> Shows that vanishing midpoint excess forces the tail norm to vanish uniformly over finite heads.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-stop-all-radii) / [proof](#proof-stmt-stop-all-radii): Sequential contradiction and finite-tail approximation -> Extends positive averaged midpoint separation to every positive radius.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-log-finite-tree) / [proof](#proof-stmt-log-finite-tree): Logarithmic supermartingale drift and centered increments -> Provides an explicit positive root gap from nonzero leaf energy, uniformly over finite trees.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-log-tail-gap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-log-tail-gap): Restrict to the first-exit boundary and apply the upper-field gap -> Converts the logarithmic upper-field gap into a finite-head midpoint gap for the joined-root sum.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-log-lens-gap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-log-lens-gap): Finite-dimensional clustering and tail approximation -> Bounds the center norm away from the unit sphere for an infinite separated family of midpoint perturbations.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-var-space) / [proof](#proof-stmt-var-space): Level estimates, norm equivalence, and outer-sum duality -> Identifies the component completion and establishes reflexivity, separable duals, finite-support density, and bounded finite projections.
- **R12** [claim](#stmt-var-no-auc) / [proof](#proof-stmt-var-no-auc): Bounded path indicators and weakly null child coordinates -> Excludes equivalent AUC norms for the variable-exponent outer sum by bounded paths with weakly null increments.
- **R13** [claim](#stmt-var-sixth-component) / [proof](#proof-stmt-var-sixth-component): Normalized boundary propagation and Jensen iteration -> Controls the sixth power of a component tail by the excess sum of squared endpoint norms.
- **R14** [claim](#stmt-var-sixth-stability) / [proof](#proof-stmt-var-sixth-stability): Weighted Jensen aggregation over components -> Aggregates the sixth-power component bound over the outer Hilbert sum without height-dependent constants.
- **R15** [claim](#stmt-var-cubic-component) / [proof](#proof-stmt-var-cubic-component): Accumulate exit mass and apply Hölder across levels -> Accumulates exit energy across levels to obtain a cubic component midpoint estimate.
- **R16** [claim](#stmt-var-cubic-stability) / [proof](#proof-stmt-var-cubic-stability): Component midpoint bounds and Hölder across components -> Aggregates the cubic component estimate into a ball-radius bound for finite heads and completed tails.
- **R17** [claim](#stmt-var-cubic-weak-null) / [proof](#proof-stmt-var-cubic-weak-null): Finite-rank removal of weakly null head coordinates -> Removes the finite-support restriction on the center for weakly null midpoint perturbations.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-fields**: The fields encode the vector as their difference, dominate their child Euclidean norms, and have a tight child inequality at every node.
- **I-root**: The root-sum convention adds the root fields; the fixed zero-root convention takes their common value.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-root**: Reuse the same numerical root cost for the fixed and free joining-root constructions. -> The root normalizations differ even on the zero-coordinate subspace.
- **A-auc**: Infer an equivalent AUC norm from the midpoint estimates or reflexivity. -> The stated spaces have a renorming obstruction.
- **A-finite**: Treat finite recursion checks as certification of the completed-space asymptotic results. -> A finite computation does not establish the subspace quantifiers or weak nullity used in the proofs.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The finite-support Euclidean field recursion gives a constructive leaf-to-root calculation. Execution checks this calculation on an exact integral subdomain; the completed-space midpoint and renorming results remain outside its coverage.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON batch of finite trees, parent indices and signed integer coordinates.
# PRECONDITIONS: At most 2000 trees; 1..7 nodes each; root parent -1; other parents precede children; coordinates in [-2,2]; all child squared sums are perfect squares.
# OUTPUTS: All least nonnegative field pairs and the root sum, as exact integers.
# TIME: Linear in the total nodes, apart from integer arithmetic.
# SPACE: Linear in the total nodes and serialized output.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact python-flint integers and certified integer square roots; no floating point.
# FAILURE: Invalid shape, out-of-contract coordinates or non-square child sum raises ValueError.
import json,sys
from flint import fmpz
batch=json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
if not isinstance(batch,list) or not 1<=len(batch)<=2000: raise ValueError('batch')
answers=[]
for case in batch:
    parent=case['parent']; values=case['values']; n=len(values)
    if len(parent)!=n or not 1<=n<=7 or parent[0]!=-1: raise ValueError('tree')
    if any(type(v)!=int or abs(v)>2 for v in values): raise ValueError('coordinates')
    children=[[] for _ in range(n)]
    for j in range(1,n):
        if type(parent[j])!=int or not 0<=parent[j]<j: raise ValueError('parent')
        children[parent[j]].append(j)
    P=[fmpz(0) for _ in range(n)]; Q=P.copy()
    for s in reversed(range(n)):
        aa=sum((P[c]*P[c] for c in children[s]),fmpz(0))
        bb=sum((Q[c]*Q[c] for c in children[s]),fmpz(0))
        if not aa.is_square() or not bb.is_square(): raise ValueError('non-square')
        A=aa.sqrt(); B=bb.sqrt()
        P[s]=max(A,values[s]+B)
        Q[s]=max(B,-values[s]+A)
    answers.append({'fields':[[int(P[j]),int(Q[j])] for j in range(n)],'root_sum':int(P[0]+Q[0])})
print(json.dumps(answers,separators=(',',':')))
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The finite-support recursion can be implemented as a norm evaluator when the represented tree, real coordinates, child aggregation, and root convention match the construction. Software correctness and arithmetic error guarantees are ABSENT. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The source models norms and midpoint perturbations, with no game dynamics, physical simulation model, or time evolution. A mapping to simulation state is ABSENT. |
| tools | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | A mathematical exploration tool could evaluate the finite recursion and inspect field constraints. This is an inferred use requiring agreement with the source recurrence; a validated numerical tool is ABSENT. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | implementable | inferred | Upward computation on the finite predecessor closure supplies a constructive evaluation procedure for the specified fields. Its transfer is limited to this recurrence; a bit-complexity analysis is ABSENT. |
| data_structures | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | A finite rooted representation of the predecessor closure can store the coordinates and fields needed by the upward procedure. The source gives the mathematical representation; a storage layout or update algorithm is ABSENT. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The source provides field recursions and geometric estimates but no hardware execution model, memory hierarchy, or accelerator mapping. Hardware-specific performance is ABSENT. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Least feasible nonnegative fields and norm estimates provide no security game, adversarial model, or hardness assumption. A cryptographic application is ABSENT. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The source constructs Banach norms and proves midpoint estimates, without a learning objective, statistical model, or training procedure. A connection to learning performance is ABSENT. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Coordinatewise minimality solves the local feasibility rule for the prescribed fields, but an operations-research objective, decision model, and solver guarantee are ABSENT. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The tree indexes coordinates in a norm construction; it does not specify distributed state, communication, failures, or a consistency protocol. A distributed-system application is ABSENT. |
| other (Banach-space geometry and renorming theory) | True | strong | inspirational | stated | The paper directly studies Banach-space geometry and renorming: completed tree norms have quantitative midpoint estimates while equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norms are excluded. This is a mathematical application; an additional engineering deployment is ABSENT. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L-real** (hypothesis): The constructions use real scalars. Blocked use: ['Transfer to complex scalars without a separate argument.']
- **L-tight** (hypothesis): The Euclidean energy propagation requires nonnegative upper fields, equal nonnegative increments, and a tight base child inequality at every internal node. Blocked use: ['Apply the energy estimate to arbitrary child aggregation or fields lacking local tightness.']
- **L-boundary** (hypothesis): The boundary energy estimate requires both base fields to vanish on the selected leaves. Blocked use: ['Use the stated boundary estimate when the base fields remain nonzero on those leaves.']
- **L-root** (hypothesis): The root sum and the common zero-root field use different normalizations and therefore different constants. Blocked use: ['Replace the common-root norm by the root-sum norm while retaining its constants.']
- **L-unit** (hypothesis): The root-sum cubic defect lemma uses a finitely supported unit center and a perturbation vanishing on a finite initial set containing its support. Blocked use: ['Apply the normalized defect inequality to an arbitrary center or overlapping head and tail without establishing the required reduction.']
- **L-radius** (hypothesis): The displayed cubic lower bound for the root-sum averaged modulus is stated in the indicated small-radius regime. Blocked use: ['Extend the same cubic formula to all positive radii using positivity alone.']
- **L-asymptotic** (formal_scope): The averaged asymptotic modulus concerns infinite-dimensional Banach spaces and closed finite-codimensional subspaces. Blocked use: ['Identify a finite tree computation with a certificate of the asymptotic modulus or a renormability theorem.']
- **L-reflexive** (counterexample_scope): Reflexivity of the joined constructions does not imply an equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm. Blocked use: ['Infer AUC renormability from reflexivity or positive midpoint modulus.']
- **L-heights** (hypothesis): The variable aggregation exponent is constant inside each height component and follows the specified height schedule. Blocked use: ['Use the variable-exponent estimates for arbitrary node-dependent exponents or weights.']
- **L-support** (hypothesis): The global variable-exponent estimates require finite total support of the center and perturbations vanishing on the chosen ancestral sets in its nonzero components. Blocked use: ['Use the finite-head estimate without its support and vanishing hypotheses.']
- **L-zero** (edge_case): The sixth-power estimate assigns a zero value to a quotient whose numerator and denominator both vanish. Blocked use: ['Evaluate the displayed quotient by ordinary division in the degenerate case.']
- **L-zero-cubic** (edge_case): The component cubic formula with a denominator is restricted to a positive endpoint maximum; the zero case forces both vectors to vanish. Blocked use: ['Divide by the endpoint maximum when it vanishes.']
- **L-weak-null** (hypothesis): The arbitrary-center conclusion requires weakly null perturbations, a lower perturbation norm bound, and both endpoint bounds. Blocked use: ['Replace weak nullity by boundedness or separation alone in this conclusion.']
- **L-component** (formal_scope): The Hilbert comparisons for the Euclidean components need not be uniform in their height. Blocked use: ['Treat the component equivalence constants as uniform across the outer sum.']
- **L-limits** (approximation): The stopping proof keeps its parameters fixed while taking the sequence limit and then chooses them in the stated order. Blocked use: ['Exchange these limits without a separate uniform argument.']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 331 (`lean/docs/331.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Asymptotic midpoint uniform convexity and unbounded diamond distortion in a reflexive tree space](../../preprints/Asymptotic-midpoint-uniform-convexity-and-unbounded-diamond-distortion-in-a-reflexive-tree-space-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)
> - [Midpoint lenses in segment spaces](../../preprints/Midpoint-lenses-in-segment-spaces-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)
> - [Distortion of countably branching diamonds from midpoint and tree energies](../../preprints/Diamond-distortion-from-midpoint-and-tree-energies-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)
> - [Exact asymptotic moduli in a Daugavet subspace of $L_1$](../../preprints/Exact-asymptotic-moduli-in-a-Daugavet-subspace-of-L1-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)
> - [Midpoint convexity from bounded tree potentials and path costs](../../preprints/Midpoint-convexity-from-bounded-tree-potentials-and-path-costs-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)
> - [Independent products in real $L_1$: asymptotic midpoint convexity without AUC renormings](../../preprints/Independent-products-in-real-L1-asymptotic-midpoint-convexity-without-AUC-renormings-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)
> - [Midpoint convexity from two recursive potentials](../../preprints/Midpoint-convexity-from-two-recursive-potentials-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The paper constructs a Banach space whose midpoint geometry is asymptotically uniformly convex even though no equivalent norm is asymptotically uniformly convex in the usual one-sided sense. The formalization proves that the full dual of the specified segment-norm completion is infinite-dimensional, separable, and reflexive; its averaged midpoint modulus is at least $\sqrt{1+t^2/12}-1$ for every $t>0$, while every equivalent norm fails asymptotic uniform convexity. It also proves that an embedding of a depth-$k$ countably branching diamond has distortion at least $\sqrt{1+k/12}$.
>
> The general-forest and word-forest results use the closed span of the coordinate functionals. Their conclusions retain the finite-ancestor and equivalent-norm hypotheses, including the scale $\alpha/(2\beta)$ when $0<\alpha\le\beta$ and the new norm lies between $\alpha$ and $\beta$ times the original norm; they do not identify that span with the full dual or assert reflexivity for every forest.
>
> The paper controls midpoint lenses in Banach spaces defined by tree-segment norms. The formalization proves the following estimate in both the full dual of the finite-height forest space and the coordinate predual of the infinite-tree space. If $x$ is supported on a finite ancestral set $H$, $R\ge0$, and $\|x+y\|,\|x-y\|\le R$, then the part of $y$ outside $H$ has norm at most $2\sqrt{R^2-\|x\|^2}$.
>
> The formalization also covers selected consequences for averaged midpoint moduli and separated families of vectors, together with reflexivity and renorming obstructions. This scope concerns the segment-space results; the companion results on diamond energies remain separate.
>
> The paper studies embeddings of countably branching diamond graphs into Banach spaces built from tree segments. The formalization proves that every embedding of the depth-$k$ diamond with distortion $D$, at any positive scale, satisfies $1+k/4\le D^2$ in either of two spaces: the full dual of the completed finite-height forest space and the closed coordinate span in the dual of the infinite-tree space. The bound applies to all pairs of vertices and is unconditional.
>
> The formalization also contains selected graph deductions under explicit companion assumptions. Twenty-two of those companion inputs are assumed in these deductions; the diamond distortion bound above does not require them.
>
> The paper exhibits a real $L_1$ subspace with positive averaged midpoint convexity but no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm. For every closed infinite-dimensional subspace whose unit ball is precompact in measure and which has the Daugavet property, the formalization computes the moduli at every unit vector: the averaged midpoint modulus is $\max(t/2,t-1)$ and the usual one-sided asymptotic modulus is $\max(0,t-2)$ for every $t>0$. The same formulas hold after taking the infimum over unit vectors, and every equivalent norm fails asymptotic uniform convexity.
>
> The formalization also constructs such a subspace on a countable product of unit intervals and proves that it has the stated measure-precompactness and Daugavet properties, so the modulus formulas apply to an actual example.
>
> The paper constructs Banach spaces from bounded tree potentials and path costs to separate averaged midpoint convexity from asymptotic uniform convexity. For the specified tree-potential completions, the formalization proves completeness, separability, infinite dimension, and an averaged midpoint modulus of at least $\sqrt{1+t^2/4}-1$ for $0<t<1$. No space linearly isomorphic to one of these completions is asymptotically uniformly convex.
>
> The formalization also covers selected path-cost duality and comparison results, clipping and energy estimates, and further renorming obstructions. These retain their stated support, head, and tail hypotheses; the remaining auxiliary assertions of the paper are outside this scope.
>
> The paper forms a real $L_1$ space from the closed span of products of independent multipliers along tree paths. For a positive, nonconstant multiplier of mean one and finite second moment, the formalization proves that this space is infinite-dimensional and has no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm.
>
> For every $t>0$, the averaged midpoint modulus nevertheless has explicit positive lower bounds. If $G$ is a standard real Gaussian, the exponential-multiplier bound is $\frac{7t}{480}\mathbb E[(|G|-20/t)_+]$, where $u_+=\max(u,0)$. For squared-Gaussian multipliers, the bounds are $\frac18\Pr(|G|\ge4\sqrt2(2+\sqrt2)/t)$ and $\frac14\Pr(|G|\ge8\sqrt6/t)$.
>
> The paper constructs three Banach spaces from recursive tree potentials: a root-sum space, a zero-root space, and a joined space. The formalization proves that all three are infinite-dimensional and have no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm. The root-sum and joined spaces have averaged midpoint modulus at least $t^3/128$ for $0<t<1$, and the root-sum modulus is positive for every $t>0$. The zero-root estimate retains its finite ancestral support and tail hypotheses, and the zero-root and joined spaces are reflexive.
>
> The formalization also covers a separated-midpoint result for the joined space and a variable-exponent model with its recursive norm, duality, projections, renorming obstruction, and sixth- and third-order stability estimates. The separation, sequence, support, and head assumptions in these results are essential parts of their statements.

```lean
class AggregationVector.LpConstruction : Prop where
  bounds : ∀ {ι : Type uIndex} [DecidableEq ι] (T : FiniteRootedTree ι)
    (e : ChildExponent), 1 < e.val →
    (∀ c, c ≠ T.root → T.depth c = T.depth (T.parent c) + 1) →
    ∀ h : ℕ, (∀ s, T.depth s ≤ h) → ∀ v : AggregationVector T e,
    ‖v‖ ≤ (2*(h+1:ℝ)^(1-1/e.val))*‖toLpLinear T e v‖ ∧
      ‖toLpLinear T e v‖ ≤ (h+1:ℝ)^(1/e.val)*‖v‖
  dense : ∀ {ι : Type uIndex} [DecidableEq ι] (T : FiniteRootedTree ι)
    (e : ChildExponent), DenseRange (toLpLinear T e)
```

def AggregationVector.toLp (hp : 1 < e.val)
    (hstep : ∀ c, c ≠ T.root → T.depth c = T.depth (T.parent c) + 1)
    (h : ℕ) (hh : ∀ s, T.depth s ≤ h) : AggregationVector T e →L[ℝ] CoordinateLp ι e
```

```lean
structure RootSumCompletionStatement {ι : Type uIndex} [DecidableEq ι]
    (T : FiniteRootedTree ι) : Prop where
  complete : CompleteSpace (TreeSpace T)
  infinite_dimensional : ¬Module.Finite ℝ (TreeSpace T)
  coordinate_continuous : ∀ s, Continuous (TreeSpace.coordinate T s)
  coordinate_extends : ∀ s (v : TreeVector T), TreeSpace.coordinate T s (v : TreeSpace T) = v s
  fields_continuous : ∀ s, Continuous (TreeSpace.P T s) ∧ Continuous (TreeSpace.Q T s)
  fields_extend : ∀ s (v : TreeVector T),
    TreeSpace.P T s (v : TreeSpace T) = T.P v s ∧ TreeSpace.Q T s (v : TreeSpace T) = T.Q v s
  root_formula : ∀ x : TreeSpace T, ‖x‖ = TreeSpace.P T T.root x + TreeSpace.Q T T.root x
  projections : ∀ (H : Finset ι) (hH : T.Initial H),
    (∀ x : TreeSpace T, ‖TreeSpace.projection T H hH x‖ ≤ ‖x‖) ∧
    (∀ x : TreeSpace T, TreeSpace.projection T H hH (TreeSpace.projection T H hH x) = TreeSpace.projection T H hH x) ∧
    (∀ (s : ι) (x : TreeSpace T), TreeSpace.coordinate T s (TreeSpace.projection T H hH x) =
      if s ∈ H then TreeSpace.coordinate T s x else 0) ∧
    FiniteDimensional ℝ (TreeSpace.projection T H hH).range ∧
    IsClosed (TreeSpace.tail T H hH : Set (TreeSpace T)) ∧
    Module.Finite ℝ (TreeSpace T ⧸ TreeSpace.tail T H hH) ∧
    ∀ x ∈ TreeSpace.tail T H hH, ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε →
      ∃ v : TreeVector T, TreeVector.projection T H hH v = 0 ∧ ‖(v : TreeSpace T)-x‖ < ε
```

```lean
structure ZeroRootCompletionStatement {ι : Type uIndex} [DecidableEq ι]
    (T : FiniteRootedTree ι) : Prop where
  complete : CompleteSpace (ZeroTreeSpace T)
  infinite_dimensional : ¬Module.Finite ℝ (ZeroTreeSpace T)
  coordinate_continuous : ∀ s, Continuous (ZeroTreeSpace.coordinate T s)
  coordinate_extends : ∀ s (v : ZeroTreeVector T),
    ZeroTreeSpace.coordinate T s (v : ZeroTreeSpace T) = ZeroTreeVector.toTree T v s
  fields_continuous : ∀ s, Continuous (ZeroTreeSpace.P T s) ∧ Continuous (ZeroTreeSpace.Q T s)
  fields_extend : ∀ s (v : ZeroTreeVector T),
    ZeroTreeSpace.P T s (v : ZeroTreeSpace T) = T.P (ZeroTreeVector.toTree T v) s ∧
    ZeroTreeSpace.Q T s (v : ZeroTreeSpace T) = T.Q (ZeroTreeVector.toTree T v) s
  root_fixed : ∀ x : ZeroTreeSpace T, ZeroTreeSpace.coordinate T T.root x = 0
  root_formula : ∀ x : ZeroTreeSpace T, ‖x‖ = ZeroTreeSpace.P T T.root x ∧ ‖x‖ = ZeroTreeSpace.Q T T.root x
  projections : ∀ (H : Finset ι) (hH : T.Initial H),
    (∀ x : ZeroTreeSpace T, ‖ZeroTreeSpace.projection T H hH x‖ ≤ ‖x‖) ∧
    (∀ x : ZeroTreeSpace T, ZeroTreeSpace.projection T H hH (ZeroTreeSpace.projection T H hH x) = ZeroTreeSpace.projection T H hH x) ∧
    (∀ (s : ι) (x : ZeroTreeSpace T), ZeroTreeSpace.coordinate T s (ZeroTreeSpace.projection T H hH x) =
      if s ∈ H then ZeroTreeSpace.coordinate T s x else 0) ∧
    FiniteDimensional ℝ (ZeroTreeSpace.projection T H hH).range ∧
    IsClosed (ZeroTreeSpace.tail T H hH : Set (ZeroTreeSpace T)) ∧
    Module.Finite ℝ (ZeroTreeSpace T ⧸ ZeroTreeSpace.tail T H hH) ∧
    ∀ x ∈ ZeroTreeSpace.tail T H hH, ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε →
      ∃ v : ZeroTreeVector T, ZeroTreeVector.projection T H hH v = 0 ∧ ‖(v : ZeroTreeSpace T)-x‖ < ε
```

```lean
structure Variable.SourceNormStatement (h : ℕ+) : Prop where
  nonneg : ∀ v : AggregationVector.CoordinateLp (ComponentNode h) (exponent h), 0 ≤ nu h v
  definite : ∀ v : AggregationVector.CoordinateLp (ComponentNode h) (exponent h), nu h v = 0 ↔ v = 0
  triangle : ∀ v w : AggregationVector.CoordinateLp (ComponentNode h) (exponent h),
    nu h (v+w) ≤ nu h v + nu h w
  homogeneous : ∀ (a : ℝ) (v : AggregationVector.CoordinateLp (ComponentNode h) (exponent h)),
    nu h (a • v) = |a| * nu h v
```

      "statement_lean": "class AggregationVector.LpConstruction : Prop where\n  bounds : ∀ {ι : Type uIndex} [DecidableEq ι] (T : FiniteRootedTree ι)\n    (e : ChildExponent), 1 < e.val →\n    (∀ c, c ≠ T.root → T.depth c = T.depth (T.parent c) + 1) →\n    ∀ h : ℕ, (∀ s, T.depth s ≤ h) → ∀ v : AggregationVector T e,\n    ‖v‖ ≤ (2*(h+1:ℝ)^(1-1/e.val))*‖toLpLinear T e v‖ ∧\n      ‖toLpLinear T e v‖ ≤ (h+1:ℝ)^(1/e.val)*‖v‖\n  dense : ∀ {ι : Type uIndex} [DecidableEq ι] (T : FiniteRootedTree ι)\n    (e : ChildExponent), DenseRange (toLpLinear T e)"

The formalization proves that the full dual of the specified segment-norm completion is infinite-dimensional, separable, and reflexive; its averaged midpoint modulus is at least $\\sqrt{1+t^2/12}-1$ for every $t>0$, while every equivalent norm fails asymptotic uniform convexity. It also proves that an embedding of a depth-$k$ countably branching diamond has distortion at least $\\sqrt{1+k/12}$.\n\nThe general-forest and word-forest results use the closed span of the coordinate functionals. Their conclusions retain the finite-ancestor and equivalent-norm hypotheses, including the scale $\\alpha/(2\\beta)$ when $0<\\alpha\\le\\beta$ and the new norm lies between $\\alpha$ and $\\beta$ times the original norm; they do not identify that span with the full dual or assert reflexivity for every forest.\n\nThe paper controls midpoint lenses in Banach spaces defined by tree-segment norms. The formalization proves the following estimate in both the full dual of the finite-height forest space and the coordinate predual of the infinite-tree space. If $x$ is supported on a finite ancestral set $H$, $R\\ge0$, and $\\|x+y\\|,\\|x-y\\|\\le R$, then the part of $y$ outside $H$ has norm at most $2\\sqrt{R^2-\\|x\\|^2}$.\n\nThe formalization also covers selected consequences for averaged midpoint moduli and separated families of vectors, together with reflexivity and renorming obstructions. This scope concerns the segment-space results; the companion results on diamond energies remain separate.\n\nThe paper studies embeddings of countably branching diamond graphs into Banach spaces built from tree segments. The formalization proves that every embedding of the depth-$k$ diamond with distortion $D$, at any positive scale, satisfies $1+k/4\\le D^2$ in either of two spaces: the full dual of the completed finite-height forest space and the closed coordinate span in the dual of the infinite-tree space. The bound applies to all pairs of vertices and is unconditional.\n\nThe formalization also contains selected graph deductions under explicit companion assumptions. Twenty-two of those companion inputs are assumed in these deductions; the diamond distortion bound above does not require them.\n\nThe paper exhibits a real $L_1$ subspace with positive averaged midpoint convexity but no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm. For every closed infinite-dimensional subspace whose unit ball is precompact in measure and which has the Daugavet property, the formalization computes the moduli at every unit vector: the averaged midpoint modulus is $\\max(t/2,t-1)$ and the usual one-sided asymptotic modulus is $\\max(0,t-2)$ for every $t>0$. The same formulas hold after taking the infimum over unit vectors, and every equivalent norm fails asymptotic uniform convexity.\n\nThe formalization also constructs such a subspace on a countable product of unit intervals and proves that it has the stated measure-precompactness and Daugavet properties, so the modulus formulas apply to an actual example.\n\nThe paper constructs Banach spaces from bounded tree potentials and path costs to separate averaged midpoint convexity from asymptotic uniform convexity. For the specified tree-potential completions, the formalization proves completeness, separability, infinite dimension, and an averaged midpoint modulus of at least $\\sqrt{1+t^2/4}-1$ for $0<t<1$. No space linearly isomorphic to one of these completions is asymptotically uniformly convex.\n\nThe formalization also covers selected path-cost duality and comparison results, clipping and energy estimates, and further renorming obstructions. These retain their stated support, head, and tail hypotheses; the remaining auxiliary assertions of the paper are outside this scope.\n\nThe paper forms a real $L_1$ space from the closed span of products of independent multipliers along tree paths. For a positive, nonconstant multiplier of mean one and finite second moment, the formalization proves that this space is infinite-dimensional and has no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm.\n\nFor every $t>0$, the averaged midpoint modulus nevertheless has explicit positive lower bounds. If $G$ is a standard real Gaussian, the exponential-multiplier bound is $\\frac{7t}{480}\\mathbb E[(|G|-20/t)_+]$, where $u_+=\\max(u,0)$. For squared-Gaussian multipliers, the bounds are $\\frac18\\Pr(|G|\\ge4\\sqrt2(2+\\sqrt2)/t)$ and $\\frac14\\Pr(|G|\\ge8\\sqrt6/t)$.\n\nThe paper constructs three Banach spaces from recursive tree potentials: a root-sum space, a zero-root space, and a joined space. The formalization proves that all three are infinite-dimensional and have no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm. The root-sum and joined spaces have averaged midpoint modulus at least $t^3/128$ for $0<t<1$, and the root-sum modulus is positive for every $t>0$. The zero-root estimate retains its finite ancestral support and tail hypotheses, and the zero-root and joined spaces are reflexive.\n\nThe formalization also covers a separated-midpoint result for the joined space and a variable-exponent model with its recursive norm, duality, projections, renorming obstruction, and sixth- and third-order stability estimates. The separation, sequence, support, and head assumptions in these results are essential parts of their statements."

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We study tree norms computed by two least nonnegative fields whose difference is the vector. For Euclidean child aggregation, two root-sum spaces have an averaged asymptotic midpoint modulus of at least $`t^3/128`$ for $`0\lt t\lt 1`$, including a reflexive joining-root space. A reflexive construction with a fixed zero root also satisfies a homogeneous cubic estimate. These spaces admit no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm. We also obtain sixth-power and cubic estimates when the aggregation exponent depends on the height of a finite component.

<a id="rec:intro"></a>
### Introduction

A recursively defined norm can be easy to evaluate at one node while its estimates deteriorate as the tree grows. We study a recursion in which two nonnegative fields encode a vector as their difference. Each field at a node dominates the norm of its values at the children, and the recursion chooses the least feasible pair. For Euclidean child aggregation, one of the two inequalities is consequently tight at every node. We show that this local equality controls midpoint perturbations uniformly in the height and branching of the tree.

<a id="intro:modulus"></a>
For an infinite-dimensional real Banach space $X$ with norm $N$, let $\operatorname{cof}(X)$ denote its closed finite-codimensional linear subspaces. The averaged asymptotic midpoint modulus is $$\label{intro:modulus}
 \widehat\delta_N(t)=\inf_{N(x)=1}\sup_{F\in\operatorname{cof}(X)}
 \inf_{\substack{y\in F\\N(y)\ge1}}
 \left(\frac{N(x+ty)+N(x-ty)}2-1\right),\qquad t>0.$$ It measures the mean increase in the endpoint norms when a perturbation avoids a suitable finite-dimensional part of the space. The usual asymptotic midpoint modulus $m_N(t)$ uses the maximum of the two endpoint norms, with unit directions, in the same infimum and supremum. The norm is *asymptotically midpoint uniformly convex*, abbreviated AMUC, if $m_N(t)>0$ for every $t>0$. This condition was introduced by Dilworth, Kutzarova, Randrianarivony, Revalski, and Zhivkov (Dilworth et al. 2016, Definition 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[DKRRZ2016](#ref-DKRRZ2016)]. The one-sided modulus is <a id="intro:auc"></a>$$\label{intro:auc}
 \overline\delta_N(t)=\inf_{N(x)=1}\sup_{F\in\operatorname{cof}(X)}
 \inf_{\substack{y\in F\\N(y)=1}}\bigl(N(x+ty)-1\bigr).$$ A norm is asymptotically uniformly convex, abbreviated AUC, if this last quantity is positive for every $t>0$.

<a id="intro:comparisons"></a>
The averaged and maximum midpoint moduli have the same positivity property, with $$\label{intro:comparisons}
 \tfrac12m_N(t)\le\widehat\delta_N(t)\le m_N(t).$$ Indeed, average is at most maximum. For the converse, at a fixed unit center intersect a witnessing subspace with the kernel of a norming functional. Both endpoint norms are then at least one, and their average excess is at least half the maximum excess. Taking the infima and suprema proves <a href="#intro:comparisons" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:comparisons">[intro:comparisons]</a>. For a fixed direction, the mean of the two endpoint norms is an even convex function of the radius. It is therefore nondecreasing on the positive half-line. Thus the inner infimum in <a href="#intro:modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:modulus">[intro:modulus]</a> can equally use $N(y)=1$, and $\widehat\delta_N$ is nondecreasing. In particular, positivity for $0<t<1$ implies positivity at every radius.

The distinction from AUC renormability is already established. Dilworth and coauthors separated the two norm properties on $\ell_2$ and posed the corresponding renorming question (Dilworth et al. 2016, Theorem 2.4 and Section 5) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[DKRRZ2016](#ref-DKRRZ2016)]. Baudier answered the general question negatively by using the Kadets–Werner modification of the Bourgain–Rosenthal construction (Baudier 2026, Theorem 1 and Corollary 1) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Baudier2026](#ref-Baudier2026)]; the underlying construction is due to Kadets and Werner (Kadets and Werner 2004, Theorem 2.5) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[KW2004](#ref-KW2004)]. That example is nonreflexive (Kadets and Werner 2004, Corollary 2.6) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[KW2004](#ref-KW2004)]. Our focus is the quantitative geometry of norms given by an explicit tree recursion. The joining-root versions are reflexive and still admit no equivalent AUC norm.

Tree norms have a substantial history in Banach-space theory. James’s construction uses sums over disjoint segments of a binary tree (James 1974, 739) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[James1974](#ref-James1974)]; Girardi proved AUC for its canonical predual and full dual (Girardi 2001, Theorems 3 and 5) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Girardi2001](#ref-Girardi2001)]. A closer comparison is the positive tree cone recalled by Banakh, following Ghoussoub, Maurey, and Schachermayer (Banakh 2000, sec. 2.I, pp. 28–29) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Banakh2000](#ref-Banakh2000)]: a nonnegative field at a node dominates the Euclidean norm of its children. That construction uses interpolation after taking a symmetric convex hull. Here the vector is the difference of two least such fields, and the norm is an explicit cost at the root. The defining norm and its completion are proved directly below.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### Results and proof structure.

Section <a href="#rec:norms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:norms">2</a> defines three Euclidean constructions, proves their completion and finite-coordinate projection properties, and states Theorem <a href="#rec:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:main">[rec:main]</a>. The first norm takes the sum of the root fields on the infinite tree. The second joins finite-height components at a zero coordinate and takes the common root field; it satisfies a homogeneous cubic inequality. The third keeps a free coordinate at that joining root and takes the sum. Both root-sum norms have averaged modulus at least $t^3/128$ for $0<t<1$; the joined space is reflexive. Distinguishing the root normalizations is essential to the constants.

The shortest quantitative argument is in Section <a href="#rec:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:energy">3</a>. Averaging the fields at $x+z$ and $x-z$ increases both fields of $x$ by the same nonnegative amount $d_s$ at node $s$. Writing $P_s,Q_s$ for the fields of $x$, the expression $$[d_s(2P_s+d_s)(2Q_s+d_s)]^{2/3}$$ dominates the sum of its values at the children, by Hölder’s inequality and the tight child inequality. Iterating to the first nodes outside a finite initial set containing the support of the center controls the complete tail by the defect at the root. No factor is multiplied once per level. Section <a href="#rec:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:obstruction">4</a> supplies the bounded-path argument that excludes AUC renormings.

Section <a href="#rec:martingale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:martingale">[rec:martingale]</a> gives a second way to propagate the same root-sum defect. A finite stopping rule separates paths according to the smaller field, a product of field ratios, and the local increase. An absorbing martingale controls the small-product event. The proof is uniform over the finite head and retains the order of its parameter limits. Section <a href="#log:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="log:section">6</a> gives a different argument on the joined tree: a logarithmic transform of two bounded supermartingales bounds a product of ratios and proves a gap for infinite separated midpoint families. The stopping proof uses a predictable martingale transform, a classical tool studied by Burkholder (Burkholder 1966) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Burkholder1966](#ref-Burkholder1966)]. In the logarithmic proof, the selected supermartingale increments split into predictable drift and centered martingale differences. The exact finite square identities and stopping estimates used here are proved in place. These arguments explain distinct mechanisms for uniformity, even though the cubic bound already proves positive midpoint modulus.

Finally, Section <a href="#var:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:section">[var:section]</a> replaces Euclidean aggregation inside a height-$h$ component by $\ell_{1+1/h}$ aggregation. This exponent is constant inside each component. Two propagation arguments control different energies: normalized iteration yields a sixth-power estimate, while accumulated layer energy yields a cubic estimate with coefficient $(6e)^{-3}$. Their complete proofs include the outer $\ell_2$ sum and the precise finite-head and weakly null tail statements. The cubic estimate also gives a cubic lower bound for the averaged midpoint modulus of this variable-exponent space.

<a id="rec:norms"></a>
### The fields, the roots, and the completed spaces

<a id="rec:definition"></a>
Our first objective is to define the norms to which the estimates apply. All scalars are real. A rooted tree has a distinguished root, and every node has finitely many predecessors. Its set of children is denoted by $\operatorname{ch}(s)$. We allow countably many children. For a finitely supported vector $v=(v_s)$, set the fields to zero below its support and compute upwards: $$\begin{aligned}
 A_s(v)&=\Bigl(\sum_{c\in\operatorname{ch}(s)}P_c(v)^2\Bigr)^{1/2},&
 B_s(v)&=\Bigl(\sum_{c\in\operatorname{ch}(s)}Q_c(v)^2\Bigr)^{1/2},\notag\\
 P_s(v)&=\max\{A_s(v),v_s+B_s(v)\},&
 Q_s(v)&=\max\{B_s(v),-v_s+A_s(v)\}.
 \label{rec:definition}
\end{aligned}$$ Only the finite predecessor closure of the support has nonzero fields. Empty sums are zero. Thus this instruction is independent of the depth at which the upward computation starts. At every node, <a id="rec:field-properties"></a>$$\label{rec:field-properties}
 P_s(v)-Q_s(v)=v_s,\qquad P_s(v)\ge A_s(v),\qquad Q_s(v)\ge B_s(v),$$ and at least one of the last two inequalities is an equality. Indeed, subtracting $v_s$ from the formula for $P_s$ gives the formula for $Q_s$. If the maximum for $P_s$ selects its first entry, its inequality is tight; otherwise the inequality for $Q_s$ is tight. Induction also shows that this is the coordinatewise least nonnegative pair with the prescribed difference and child lower bounds.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Three Euclidean constructions

The local rule is the same in the following spaces, but the underlying trees and the values taken at the root differ.

1.  On $T=\mathbb N^{<\omega}$, including the root $o=\varnothing$, put $$N_\Sigma(v)=P_o(v)+Q_o(v),\qquad v\in c_{00}(T),$$ and let $X_\Sigma$ be the completion.

2.  Let $T_n=\bigcup_{j=0}^n\mathbb N^j$, $n\ge1$, be disjoint copies with roots $r_n$. Add a root $\rho$ whose children are the $r_n$, obtaining a tree $\mathcal J$. For vectors on $\mathcal J\setminus\{\rho\}$ set $v_\rho=0$. Then $P_\rho(v)=Q_\rho(v)$; define $$N_0(v)=P_\rho(v)=Q_\rho(v),$$ and let $X_0$ be the completion.

3.  <span id="rec:joined-sum-definition" label="rec:joined-sum-definition"></span> On the same tree $\mathcal J$, now allowing the coordinate $v_\rho$ to vary, put $$N_J(v)=P_\rho(v)+Q_\rho(v),\qquad v\in c_{00}(\mathcal J),$$ and let $X_J$ be the completion.

In particular, $N_J(v)=2N_0(v)$ on the subspace where $v_\rho=0$. Allowing that coordinate to vary produces an additional one-dimensional part; it does not change the local recursion.

We call a finite root-containing set *initial* if it contains all predecessors of its nodes, write $\Pi_H$ for coordinate restriction, and put $F_H=\ker\Pi_H$ in the corresponding completion. The next theorem gives the principal quantitative conclusions. The definition of $\widehat\delta$ is <a href="#intro:modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:modulus">[intro:modulus]</a>.

<a id="stmt-rec-joined-root-stability"></a>
<div id="rec:joined-root-stability" class="theorem">

<a id="rec:sum-modulus"></a>
<a id="rec:equal-estimate"></a>
**Theorem 1**. *<span id="rec:main" label="rec:main"></span>For $N=N_\Sigma$ on $X_\Sigma$, or $N=N_J$ on $X_J$, one has $$\label{rec:sum-modulus}
 \widehat\delta_N(t)\ge t^3/128\qquad(0<t<1).$$ For $X_0$, a finite initial set $H$, a vector $x$ supported in $H$, and $z\in F_H\setminus\{0\}$, one has $$\label{rec:equal-estimate}
 \frac{N_0(x+z)+N_0(x-z)}2\ge N_0(x)
 +\frac{N_0(z)^3}{8(2N_0(x)+N_0(z))^2}.$$ Each of $X_\Sigma,X_0,X_J$ admits no equivalent AUC norm.*

</div>

The energy proof in Section <a href="#rec:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:energy">3</a> gives the first two assertions, uniformly in the height and branching of $H$. Section <a href="#rec:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:obstruction">4</a> proves the last assertion by following bounded paths whose next increments are weakly null. The two later probabilistic arguments obtain uniform separation by controlling the propagation along random paths.

<a id="stmt-rec-completion"></a>
<div id="rec:completion" class="proposition">

**Proposition 2**. *These formulas define infinite-dimensional Banach spaces after completion. Coordinate evaluations and the fields extend continuously. If $H$ is a finite set containing the root and all predecessors of its nodes, then coordinate restriction $\Pi_H$ is a contractive projection. Its kernel $F_H$ has finite codimension, and finitely supported elements of $F_H$ are dense in $F_H$. Both $X_0$ and $X_J$ are reflexive.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rec-completion"><summary>Proof of stmt-rec-completion</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#rec:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:completion">2</a>.* The fields are positively homogeneous and subadditive. To verify subadditivity, induct upwards: the child Euclidean norms are monotone on nonnegative arrays and satisfy the triangle inequality. Each entry in either maximum in <a href="#rec:definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rec:definition">[rec:definition]</a> is therefore subadditive, and a maximum of two subadditive functions is subadditive. Negation interchanges $P_s$ and $Q_s$. This proves symmetry and the triangle inequality for each proposed norm. Moreover, $$|v_s|\le\max\{P_s(v),Q_s(v)\}\le N(v),
 \qquad N\in\{N_\Sigma,N_0,N_J\},$$ by following the child inequalities to the corresponding root. This proves definiteness and coordinate continuity. The coordinate vectors are linearly independent, so the spaces are infinite dimensional.

The fields of $\Pi_Hv$ vanish outside $H$. Inside $H$ its coordinates are unchanged, and each maximum is nondecreasing in the child fields; induction gives $P_s(\Pi_Hv)\le P_s(v)$ and the same for $Q_s$. Thus $\Pi_H$ is contractive. Ignore the fixed coordinate $\rho$ when $N=N_0$. It extends to the completion and has finite-dimensional range. Applying $I-\Pi_H$ to finite approximations proves tail density. Choose an increasing sequence $(H_k)$ of finite initial sets covering the tree. If a finite vector $v$ is supported in $H_k$, then $$N(x-\Pi_{H_k}x)
 \le N(x-v)+N(\Pi_{H_k}(v-x))\le2N(x-v).$$ Density therefore gives $\Pi_{H_k}x\to x$ for every completed vector $x$. In particular, the coordinates separate points. A completed vector whose coordinates vanish outside a finite initial set $H$ equals $\Pi_Hx$, so support in $H$ has its usual coordinate meaning. Subadditivity and sign exchange give $$|P_s(v)-P_s(w)|\le N(v-w),\qquad
 |Q_s(v)-Q_s(w)|\le N(v-w).$$ The fields therefore extend continuously, and the root norm formulas persist on the completions.

<a id="rec:block-equivalence"></a>
We prove reflexivity by describing the finite-height components. Set $M_n(v)=\max\{P_{r_n}(v),Q_{r_n}(v)\}$ on $c_{00}(T_n)$, and let $E_n$ be its completion under this norm. Writing $m_s=\max\{P_s,Q_s\}$ gives $$m_s\le |v_s|+\Bigl(\sum_{c\in\operatorname{ch}(s)}m_c^2\Bigr)^{1/2}.$$ The Euclidean norm of the $m_s$ on one level is at most the Euclidean norm of the coordinates on that level plus the corresponding norm of $m_c$ on the next level. Iteration and Cauchy–Schwarz yield $M_n(v)\le\sqrt{n+1}\,\|v\|_2$. Conversely the $\ell_2$ norm of either field on any level is at most its root value. Since $v=P-Q$, $$\label{rec:block-equivalence}
 \frac{\|v\|_2}{2\sqrt{n+1}}\le M_n(v)
 \le\sqrt{n+1}\,\|v\|_2.$$ Hence $E_n$ is $\ell_2(T_n)$ with an equivalent norm, and is reflexive. The recursion extends to its vectors by continuity or directly by the finitely many levels.

For $v$ with zero joining coordinate, put $L(v)=(\sum_n M_n(v|_{T_n})^2)^{1/2}$. The two child arrays at $\rho$ give <a id="rec:joined-equivalence"></a>$$\label{rec:joined-equivalence}
 N_0(v)\le L(v)\le\sqrt2\,N_0(v).$$ For a free root coordinate $a=v_\rho$, with the remaining coordinates written as $w$, let $A=(\sum_nP_{r_n}(w)^2)^{1/2}$ and $B=(\sum_nQ_{r_n}(w)^2)^{1/2}$. Then <a id="rec:free-root-equivalence"></a>$$\label{rec:free-root-equivalence}
 N_J(v)=\max\{2A-a,2B+a\},\qquad
 \max\{|a|,L(w)\}\le N_J(v)\le |a|+2L(w).$$ For the lower bound, $N_J\ge |a|$ and $N_J\ge A+B\ge L(w)$. These comparisons identify $X_0$ with the $\ell_2$ sum of the $E_n$ under an equivalent norm, and $X_J$ with that sum plus $\mathbb R$. The Hilbert comparisons for individual components need not be uniform in $n$: we take the outer sum in the norms $M_n$ themselves.

For completeness, an $\ell_2$ sum of Banach spaces is complete: a Cauchy sequence converges in each component, and its Cauchy bounds pass to all finite component sums and then to their supremum. Its dual is the $\ell_2$ sum of the component duals. The upper norm bound is Cauchy–Schwarz; the lower bound follows by testing a functional on finitely many almost norming component vectors with scalar coefficients proportional to their dual norms. Finite component vectors are dense. Applying this dual identification twice proves reflexivity when each component is reflexive. Equivalent norms preserve reflexivity. This proves the assertion for both joined spaces. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Component root sums.

The alternative norm $P_{r_n}+Q_{r_n}$ lies between $M_n$ and $2M_n$, so it gives the same component completion. Its behavior on individual levels can also be read directly from the recursion. For a finite vector on $T_n$, put $s_a=P_a+Q_a$. Then <a id="rec:sum-level-bounds"></a>$$\label{rec:sum-level-bounds}
 \max\{|v_a|,\|(s_c)_{c\in\operatorname{ch}(a)}\|_2\}
 \le s_a\le |v_a|+2\|(s_c)_{c\in\operatorname{ch}(a)}\|_2.$$ Indeed, $s_a\ge A_a+B_a\ge\|(s_c)_c\|_2$ and $s_a=\max\{2A_a-v_a,2B_a+v_a\}$. Iteration bounds its root value above by $\sum_{d=0}^n2^d\|v|_{\text{level }d}\|_2$, while each level norm of $v$ is at most that root value. These provide direct Hilbert comparisons for the component root-sum convention.

<a id="rec:energy"></a>
### From root defect to boundary energy

To prove Theorem <a href="#rec:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:main">[rec:main]</a>, we will compare the fields of the center with their averages at the two endpoints. The following lemma is the required calculation. It bounds the squared increases at a chosen boundary by a quantity measured only at the root.

<a id="stmt-rec-two-field"></a>
<div id="rec:two-field" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Two-field energy). *Let $J$ be a subset of the leaves of a finite tree with root $o$. Suppose that $P,Q,P',Q'$ are nonnegative fields, each satisfying $$A_s^2\ge\sum_{c\in\operatorname{ch}(s)}A_c^2$$ at every internal node, for each $A\in\{P,Q,P',Q'\}$. Suppose also that $P'-P=Q'-Q=d\ge0$, and that at each internal node at least one of the inequalities for $P,Q$ is an equality. If $P_b=Q_b=0$ for $b\in J$, then $$\sum_{b\in J}d_b^2
 \le\bigl[d_o(2P_o+d_o)(2Q_o+d_o)\bigr]^{2/3}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rec-two-field"><summary>Proof of stmt-rec-two-field</summary>

*Proof.* Put $E_s=[d_s(2P_s+d_s)(2Q_s+d_s)]^{2/3}$. We show that $E_s\ge\sum_c E_c$. Suppose first that $P_s^2=\sum_cP_c^2$, and put $$U_s=(P'_s)^2-P_s^2=d_s(2P_s+d_s),\qquad
 V_s=(Q'_s+Q_s)^2=(2Q_s+d_s)^2.$$ Use the same definitions at the children. The upper inequality for $P'$ and the equality for $P$ give $U_s\ge\sum_c U_c$. The triangle inequality in $\ell_2$ applied to the child fields $Q',Q$ gives $V_s\ge\sum_c V_c$. Consequently Hölder’s inequality yields $$\sum_c E_c=\sum_c U_c^{2/3}V_c^{1/3}
 \le\left(\sum_c U_c\right)^{2/3}
     \left(\sum_c V_c\right)^{1/3}
 \le U_s^{2/3}V_s^{1/3}=E_s.$$ If the tight field at $s$ is $Q$, interchange $P,Q$ in this calculation. At a leaf in $J$, $E_b=d_b^2$. Iterating the inequality through the finite tree and discarding the nonnegative energies of the other leaves proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rec-root-sum-stability"></a>
<div id="rec:root-sum-stability" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4**. *Let $N=N_\Sigma$ or $N=N_J$. Suppose that $x$ is a finitely supported unit vector and that $z\in F_H$, where $H$ is a finite initial set containing $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}x$. If $$d=\frac12\left(\frac{N(x+z)+N(x-z)}2-1\right),$$ then $d\ge0$ and $N(z)^3\le64d(1+d)^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rec-root-sum-stability"><summary>Proof of stmt-rec-root-sum-stability</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#rec:root-sum-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:root-sum-stability">4</a> and the quantitative assertions of Theorem <a href="#rec:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:main">[rec:main]</a>.* We use the fields from <a href="#rec:definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rec:definition">[rec:definition]</a>. For a finite head vector $x$ and a finite tail $z$ vanishing on an initial set $H$ containing $\operatorname{supp}x$, set $$P_s=P_s(x),\quad Q_s=Q_s(x),\quad
 P'_s=\tfrac12(P_s(x+z)+P_s(x-z)),\quad
 Q'_s=\tfrac12(Q_s(x+z)+Q_s(x-z)).$$ The coordinate difference identity and convexity give the same nonnegative defect $d_s=P'_s-P_s=Q'_s-Q_s$. The averaged fields satisfy the upper inequalities by the $\ell_2$ triangle inequality. At a first node $b$ outside $H$, the head fields vanish on its whole subtree, so $$d_b=\tfrac12(P_b(z)+Q_b(z)).$$ Only finitely many such values are nonzero. Apply Lemma <a href="#rec:two-field" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:two-field">3</a> to the finite relevant part of $H$ and its first-exit boundary, retaining at each boundary node its already-computed full-subtree field values. Discard only subtrees where all four fields vanish. This gives the bound on $\sum_b d_b^2$. Figure <a href="#fig:boundary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:boundary">1</a> shows the finite part through which the inequality is propagated.

<figure id="fig:boundary">

<figcaption>The shaded nodes form the finite initial set $H$. Each boundary node $b$ represents its entire descendant subtree: the head vector is zero there, while the tail fields $P_{b}(z), Q_{b}(z)$ have already been computed. The energy inequality propagates from these boundary values to the root. The omitted branches contribute zero in a finite-support calculation.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-boundary"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:boundary`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={font=\small},
 head/.style={circle,fill=blue!20,draw=blue!50!black,minimum size=5mm},
 boundary/.style={circle,fill=white,draw=black,minimum size=5mm}]
\node[head] (o) at (0,2.2) {$o$};
\node[head] (a) at (-1.6,1.1) {};
\node[head] (b) at (1.3,1.1) {};
\node[head] (c) at (-2.3,0) {};
\node[boundary] (d) at (-0.9,0) {$b_1$};
\node[boundary] (e) at (0.7,0) {$b_2$};
\node[boundary] (f) at (2.1,0) {$b_3$};
\node[boundary] (g) at (-2.9,-1.1) {$b_4$};
\node[boundary] (h) at (-1.7,-1.1) {$b_5$};
\foreach \u/\v in {o/a,o/b,a/c,a/d,b/e,b/f,c/g,c/h}
 \draw (\u)--(\v);
\foreach \v in {d,e,f,g,h} \draw[densely dotted] (\v.south)--++(0,-.55);
\node[align=left,anchor=west] at (3.05,.8) {$E_s\ge\sum_c E_c$\\[4pt]$E_b=d_b^2$};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 7 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A rooted tree diagram where internal/ancestor nodes forming the set $H$ are shaded circular nodes and boundary nodes are unshaded circular nodes. At the top is the root node labeled $o$, which branches to two shaded child nodes. The left child node branches to an unshaded leaf node labeled $b_1$ and to another shaded node; this shaded node further branches to two unshaded leaf nodes labeled $b_4$ and $b_5$. The right child node branches to two unshaded leaf nodes labeled $b_2$ and $b_3$. To the right of the tree diagram, two mathematical relations are displayed: $E_s \ge \sum_c E_c$ and $E_b = d_b^2$.
```


We next convert this energy into a norm estimate for each of the two root conventions. On $H$ the coordinate of $z$ is zero, so $P_s(z)=Q_s(z)$ there. With $r_s=\max\{P_s(z),Q_s(z)\}$ one has $r_s^2\le\sum_c r_c^2$ on $H$ and $r_b\le2d_b$ at its boundary. Thus $r_o\le2(\sum_b d_b^2)^{1/2}$.

*The sum of the root fields.* First use either root-sum space $X_\Sigma$ or $X_J$, and write $N=N_\Sigma$ or $N=N_J$, respectively. Assume $N(x)=1$ and put $$d=d_o=\frac12\left(\frac{N(x+z)+N(x-z)}2-1\right).$$ Since $N(z)=2r_o$, the energy lemma gives $$N(z)^3\le64d(2P_o+d)(2Q_o+d)\le64d(1+d)^2.$$ The last inequality uses $P_o+Q_o=1$. The field continuity and tail density from Proposition <a href="#rec:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:completion">2</a> extend this cubic inequality to every $z\in F_H$, with the finite unit center $x$ fixed. This proves Lemma <a href="#rec:root-sum-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:root-sum-stability">4</a>. If $N(z)\ge t$ with $0<t<1$, then $d\ge t^3/256$: for $d\le1$ use $(1+d)^2\le4$, and for $d>1$ the claim is immediate. Since the average gain is $2d$, it is at least $t^3/128$.

Fix either root-sum space $X\in\{X_\Sigma,X_J\}$ with its norm $N$. For any unit center $x_0\in X$ and $\varepsilon>0$, choose a finite unit center $x$ with $N(x-x_0)<\varepsilon$, then choose a finite initial $H$ containing its support. For all $y\in F_H$ with $N(y)\ge1$, put $z=ty$ in the preceding estimate. Replacing $x$ by $x_0$ loses at most $\varepsilon$ in the average, uniformly in $y$. Thus the supremum over finite-codimensional subspaces at $x_0$ is at least $t^3/128-\varepsilon$. Letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ and taking the infimum over unit $x_0$ proves <a href="#rec:sum-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rec:sum-modulus">[rec:sum-modulus]</a>.

*The common root field.* Now use $X_0$ and write $N=N_0$, with $o=\rho$ in the preceding calculation. By definition $N(v)=P_o(v)=Q_o(v)$. Put $$d=d_o=\frac{N(x+z)+N(x-z)}2-N(x).$$ Now $N(z)=r_o$, and the same energy lemma yields $$N(z)^3\le8d(2N(x)+d)^2.$$ The triangle inequality gives $d\le N(z)$. For $z\ne0$ this proves the homogeneous estimate $$\frac{N(x+z)+N(x-z)}2
 \ge N(x)+\frac{N(z)^3}{8(2N(x)+N(z))^2}.$$ The tail density from Section <a href="#rec:norms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rec:norms">2</a> and continuity extend this inequality to every nonzero completed tail, proving <a href="#rec:equal-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rec:equal-estimate">[rec:equal-estimate]</a>. ◻

</details>

The factors eight and sixty-four come from the conversion of boundary fields into the tail norm: the sum of the root fields introduces a factor two before cubing. Both estimates therefore depend on the same local energy inequality, while their root normalizations remain distinct.

*Remark (reciprocal form).* In the finite-head calculation above, let $\mathcal B$ denote the first-exit boundary, and retain the fields $P,Q$ and their averaged increments $d$. For a node $s$ of that finite tree, put $$B_s=\left(\sum_{\substack{b\in\mathcal B\\s\preceq b}}d_b^2\right)^{1/2},
 \qquad w_s^P=2P_s+d_s,\qquad w_s^Q=2Q_s+d_s.$$ Here $s\preceq b$ means that $s$ lies on the path from the root to $b$, including $s=b$.
